member of the commission.– madam president, first and foremost, i would like to thank my colleague, the rapporteur, mark demesmaeker, for his report on the biodiversity mid-term review.
it is an honour for me to contribute to this discussion on the eu’s progress towards achieving its 2020 biodiversity targets.
the european parliament’s report, which is being put forward for adoption tomorrow, delivers a strong message about the importance of biodiversity and ecosystem services for human welfare and socio-economic development, but it also delivers a message of concern about protecting this essential natural capital.
i believe that this report can help steer and accelerate progress towards the difficult task of achieving our 2020 biodiversity targets.
the commission’s biodiversity mid—term review revealed that, while we are mid-term – and here i agree completely with mr demesmaeker – we are not mid-way to achieving our targets.
we are continuing to lose biodiversity, and this has very serious implications for our ability to ensure food security, for our quality of life and sustainable socio-economic development, and for mitigating and adapting to climate change and delivering on our international commitments, including the recently-adopted agenda 2030 for sustainable development.
we need to mobilise the efforts of actors at all levels and across many sectors in order to deliver on our biodiversity commitments.
we need, in particular, to fully implement the eu nature legislation, focusing our efforts on completing the natura 2000 network, especially for the marine environment, and on ensuring effective management and financing of natura 2000 sites.
the ongoing fitness check of the eu nature legislation provoked the highest ever number of responses – over half a million – to a commission public consultation, demonstrating the level of importance of nature and biodiversity to european citizens.
here i would like to stress that the fitness check is an opportunity to consider how we can better ensure that the nature directives, which are key instruments in achieving our biodiversity goals, are both effective and efficient.
yes, the nature directives play a central and critical role in halting biodiversity loss in europe, but they are not enough on their own.
we need to do more to effectively protect and restore biodiversity and ecosystem services both within and beyond the natura 2000 network.
focusing on the positive economic gains through recognising and accounting for biodiversity will also be key.
we must deliver on the broader goals of the eu biodiversity strategy, and this will ultimately depend on society’s commitment to sustainable production and consumption.
it is very clear that we need to raise awareness about the value of nature and biodiversity, not only because nature and biodiversity are valuable in themselves and important for citizens, but also because they underpin our well-being and socio-economic development.
these sectors must therefore be at the heart of any solution to halting biodiversity loss.
there are many excellent examples of local improvements as a direct result of good practices, and these carry an important message about the achievability of the 2020 biodiversity targets.
this will go a long way towards tackling one of the main causes of biodiversity loss in the european union.
finally, at global level, we need to deliver on our international financial commitments whilst addressing the underlying causes of global biodiversity loss, including the impacts of eu consumption, production and trade.
i hope that we can do all of this together so that by 2020 we will have a positive result, which will be a significant stepping stone towards our 2030 sustainable development goals.

the 2011-2020 strategy sets six broad objectives: full implementation of the birds and habitats directives; maintenance and restoration of ecosystems and their services; increasing the contribution of agriculture and forestry to maintaining and enhancing biodiversity; sustainable use of fisheries resources; combating invasive alien species; and strengthening the eu's contribution to preventing global biodiversity loss.
it therefore calls on the commission to submit to parliament two annual reports in which the council and the commission explain the situation, the reasons for non-compliance and the strategy to ensure future compliance, and calls on both to urgently give higher priority to meeting the 2020 targets and to provide greater financial support for the protection of biodiversity.

i thank parliament for the majority support for my proposal, and my vote is a resounding yes to sustainable fishing and a much fairer allocation of bluefin tuna quotas.
this species is crucial, especially for the delicate mediterranean ecosystem, and one of the most valued in the world.
it represents environmentally sustainable fishing, although the pp government has forgotten about it in its unfair allocation of quotas.
although the importance of artisanal fishing is emphasized, specific references to a traditional and sustainable fishing technique such as cod trawling have been removed, which in my view deserves specific mention in this regulation.
the initial position of parliament, which we support, contained provisions in favour of traditional, sustainable and small-scale arts, specifically on the craft of making rugs.
the parliament's proposal would have improved the situation of the cages by increasing the quotas for the cages, which would have led to an increase in the sustainable exploitation of tuna.
‒ we voted against because, while the report underlines the role of artisanal fishing gear such as traditional traps, we believe that when implementing the recovery plan, the eu should place even greater emphasis on the use of traditional traps which contribute to the restoration of tuna stocks and have very little impact on marine ecosystems due to the low level of energy consumption and bycatch.
we also feel it necessary to point out that in practice the distribution of quotas among the member states does not apply article 17 of the cfp, which calls for the allocation to be based on 'transparent and objective criteria, including those of an environmental, social and economic nature'.

mr president, this regulation - and i have already said this - on energy efficiency labelling is undoubtedly key to providing the citizen and the consumer with the best information, the best understanding of the impact that certain products on the market can have on the energy bill, on the electricity bill, and, in turn, it is a regulation that will undoubtedly contribute to the objective of reducing emissions.
the use of household appliances is increasing and 85% of european consumers take energy labelling into account in their purchasing decisions, so it is widely accepted in all eu countries.
it is a guarantee for the consumer, while at the same time it is an instrument for achieving the objectives of reducing emissions, improving energy efficiency and reducing dependence on fuels.
it is estimated that by 2020, the fuel savings from the use of energy efficiency labels will be equivalent to 180 million tonnes of oil.

sustainable development in trade policy is an area where parliament and the commission clearly share an interest and a strong engagement.
the commission’s new trade policy, the trade for all strategy, has sustainable development at its core.
it sets out an ambitious and comprehensive agenda for the eu, underlining the fact that trade is not only about economic growth but also about defending the values that we share in europe: social justice, respect for human rights, and high labour and environmental standards.
this strategy builds upon progress already achieved since the adoption by the european parliament in 2010 of the three resolutions in this area: first, the introduction of binding and comprehensive trade and sustainable development chapters in all our free trade agreements; second, the strengthening of the human rights dimension, including gender, in our sustainable impact assessment; and, third, reforming the eu generalised system of preferences, including the flagship gsp+, sustainable development and good governance.
the eu has been a frontrunner over recent years in pursuing the integration of sustainable development into its policy, and this engagement has also been recognised by many international organisations and our trading partners.
in this regard we have set some priorities, notably the effective implementation of trade and sustainable development chapters, better linking trade policy instruments to development cooperation, including with a view to supporting the 2030 agenda, and the promotion of responsible supply chains and of private sector engagement to integrate sustainable development objectives as part of core business strategies.
the report on a new forward-looking and innovative future strategy for trade and investment underlines the importance of pushing both jobs and growth, as well as the value agenda that focused on human rights, good governance and sustainable development.
the commission welcomes the attention given to the importance of coherence between trade and other eu policies, and this new commission is working hard, along these lines, to integrate the various policy areas – respect for human and labour rights, protection of the environment, and the development of partner countries, including sustainable development chapters – in all our trade agreements.
much of what you propose is actually already being done by the commission but we can, of course, do more and we look forward to working with you in order to have not only as efficient a trade policy as possible but also a sustainable and responsible one.
but above all, there is an urgent need for a binding treaty to ensure that companies respect social and environmental standards and, above all, human rights.

‒ this report reviews the implementation of the postal services directive, adopted in 1997 and amended in 2002 and 2008. the commission's preliminary work has enabled us to include in the text a number of important points advocating a universal, accessible, long-term sustainable public postal service capable of maintaining quality jobs.
the report proposes a balance between free competition, consumer demands, the sustainability of the universal service and its financing, and the preservation of jobs.
the postal sector is called upon to ensure the sustainability of universal service, with low prices for consumers but at the same time high quality, with fast 'track and trace' services open to all operators, transparent commercial services to all other operators and at the same time with workers with good working conditions, trained and guaranteed stable employment.
this proposal is surprising, given that the fight against greenhouse gas emissions is presented as a priority by the european institutions.

‒ i am voting in favour of a text which i consider to be in the direction of building a common and standardised agenda between the union and iran, based on respect for sovereignty, multilateralism in the settlement of disputes, the joint pursuit of processes leading to progress in respect for human rights, the sustainable and sustainable development of the iranian people and peace and security for all the peoples of the middle east.
i am, however, opposed to the view of iran as a source of regional instability, to the establishment of relations guided predominantly by commercial criteria, to the presentation of trade liberalization per se as a panacea for the achievement of the development of peoples like the iranian (who participate in asymmetric and subordinate conditions in the global market) and to the use of fossil and nuclear energies as the basis of the productive and energy matrix of any country.

mr president, access to sustainable energy is one of the targets of the united nations' sustainable development goals, and this is because access to it is a key element in the achievement of people's basic rights.
energy poverty is a global problem.
in spain, more than five million people are cold in winter; the electricity bill has risen by 73% in eight years; and, according to the academy of environmental sciences, more people die each year from energy poverty than from traffic accidents.
in europe, we see that energy poverty is the result of deregulation of a strategic sector that has been left to the unlimited greed of the private sector.
in order to ensure access to energy it is essential that the state maintains its political space to regulate and ensure effective access to energy for all citizens.
so instruments like budget support and encouraging public investment are the ways to overcome energy poverty in developing countries, not to increase the role of the private sector through blended finance, because we will be exporting an energy model that is proving inefficient and, moreover, indecent.
on behalf of the green group, mr president, i was at cop 22 last week, and we clearly recalled at this climate change summit that access to energy is central to the countries of the south, especially considering that a billion people right now - and half of them in africa - do not have access to energy.
but we're not talking about just any kind of access to any kind of energy here, because we know perfectly well that there are types of energy that we can't afford right now, both for poverty reduction and for fighting climate change.
if we talk about access to energy in the countries of the south, we have to talk above all about access to energy that is sustainable; to energy that is clean; to energy that fights for social justice; that fights for climate justice; that fights to meet people's basic needs; and, also, that fights for human rights, starting with women, who are really the first to suffer from climate change.

i am referring to energy that is really adapted to the needs of these populations, especially in africa.

we also agree on another point: we must divest as soon as possible of all fossil fuels, and that money should be put as soon as possible into the fight against climate change and, moreover, in favour of the objectives of sustainable development.
